## [2026-08-12] User

> we have a 2.5 D Doom-like extension. Would it be possible to do something like a Minecraft extension because students are very excited about Minecraft. Are the any copyright issue I should be aware of? Just answer do not implement

### Claude

Short answer: technically yes, legally you need to be careful about how you frame it.

**Feasible?** Very. A voxel-world extension (place/break cubes, chunked grid, simple textures) fits the same folder-extension pattern as `raycast_2_5d` — new room-renderer + actions, no core changes. It's a bigger lift than the raycast work (chunk meshing/culling for performance, inventory UI, world persistence) but nothing exotic.

**Copyright/trademark issues to actually watch:**
- **"Minecraft" the name/logo** — trademarked by Mojang/Microsoft. Don't call it that, don't use their logo or branding, don't market it as Minecraft-compatible. Call it something generic ("Block World," "Voxel Builder").
- **Original textures/assets** — Minecraft's actual texture files (grass block, dirt, etc.) and its distinctive font are copyrighted. Don't ship or bundle real Minecraft textures/sounds. Hand-drawn/original pixel art in a similar blocky style is fine — the general *aesthetic* of low-res voxel cubes isn't ownable, only the specific expression is.
- **Mechanics/gameplay concepts** — game mechanics themselves (place/break blocks, crafting, survival loop) are not copyrightable. Clone Wars-style "blocky sandbox building game" clones are extremely common and legal (Minetest/Luanti is a well-known fully open-source example). This is the safe territory.
- **`.minecraft` world/mod file formats** — if you ever import real Minecraft world saves or resource packs, that's importing copyrighted third-party content, different risk profile than building your own format.

Bottom line: build your own voxel engine with original art and a different name, and you're in "inspired by" territory same as Minetale/Terasology/Minetest — no copyright problem. The risk only shows up if you use their name/logo or their actual asset files.

## [2026-08-12] User

> I want to steer clear of any copyright issues with Minecraft. Can we base it on Luanti?

### Claude

Architecturally, no — Luanti is a separate C++/Lua engine, not something you can import into a Python/PySide6 codebase; "basing it on Luanti" in practice would mean writing your own voxel engine (same pattern as the raycast extension) while borrowing ideas from its node-based world format, not forking its code.

Where Luanti *is* directly useful is its assets: the engine itself is LGPL (fine to be inspired by, awkward to actually embed), but block textures from Minetest Game and community packs are usually CC0 or CC-BY-SA — CC0 ones you can drop in freely, CC-BY-SA ones require attribution and share-alike (any exported student game bundling them would need to stay CC-BY-SA too, which could be a headache for a "kids export their game" tool). My recommendation: build the engine from scratch like raycast_2_5d, and only pull in specific CC0-licensed Luanti/Minetest texture packs for block art (checking each pack's license individually) rather than treating "based on Luanti" as reusing its codebase.

## [2026-08-13] User

> what is the the pitch-picking fix

### Claude

The core problem: `handlers.py`'s `_pick` (line ~63) never reads `cfg.get("pitch")` at all — it calls `pick_block` with a flat horizontal ray at a fixed layer, regardless of what `set_look_pitch` has done to the view. So tilting up/down changes what you *see* but not what `place_block`/`break_block` *target*.

The fix isn't a one-line swap, though — there's a real wrinkle I want to flag before touching it:

**What's already there to use:** `screen_ray(sx, sy, ...)` converts a screen pixel into `(angle, z_per_px)` — the same thing the preview tool uses for mouse-aim. For the crosshair (no mouse, always screen-center), the ray would be `screen_ray(screen_w/2, screen_h/2, ..., horizon=horizon_for(screen_h, pitch))`. When pitch is 0, that reduces to `z_per_px=0` — an exactly-horizontal ray, same as today. When pitch is nonzero, `z_per_px` comes out nonzero and the ray actually tilts with the view. Then `pick_voxel(room, cam_x, cam_y, eye_z, angle, z_per_px, ...)` marches it in 3D and returns `(target, placement)` in the same `(x, y, z)` shape `pick_block` already returns — so `execute_place_block_action`/`execute_break_block_action` downstream don't need to change at all.

**The wrinkle:** `pick_block` has a specific "prefer refilling a gap" rule — added after a playtest found that without it, a hole knocked in a wall could never be refilled (the placement lands somewhere past the hole instead of in it). `pick_voxel` has no such rule; it just tracks the single previous empty cell. At `z_per_px=0` those aren't the same thing whenever the ray passes over more open floor *beyond* the hole before hitting something else — `pick_block` still remembers the hole via its dedicated `gap` tracking, `pick_voxel` would forget it and place wherever the ray actually stopped. A naive swap would quietly re-break the exact bug `ecb319c` fixed.

So the real fix is: either port that gap-preference into `pick_voxel` (or a 3D-aware version of it) before switching `_pick` over, or consciously accept/document that pitched picking loses the gap heuristic. I'd also need to plumb screen width/height into `_pick` (it runs game-logic side, no direct render surface — same pattern `draw_doom_hud`'s handler already uses via `game_runner.window_width/height`).

Want me to go ahead — and if so, port the gap rule into 3D picking, or ship without it and note the tradeoff?

> 
> ## 2. Key Technical Concepts
> 
> - **PyGameMaker (pygm2) architecture**: `runtime/game_runner.py` (GameRunner, GameRoom, GameInstance, GameSprite), `runtime/action_executor.py` (ActionExecutor, `_parse_value` expression mini-language, `_eval_bool_expression` for if_condition), `events/action_types.py` (ActionType/ActionParameter schemas), `events/plugin_loader.py` (extension/plugin loading, `list_available_extensions`, `load_all_plugins`).
> - **Folder extension pattern**: `extensions/<name>/` with `extension.json` manifest (`provides_actions` must be kept in sync with real `PLUGIN_ACTIONS` — this drifted stale once this session, now has a regression test), `__init__.py` (PLUGIN_ACTIONS, PluginExecutor, PLUGIN_ROOM_RENDERERS), reaching the engine via `instance.action_executor`.
> - **Block World voxel engine** (`extensions/block_world/`): `state.py` (sparse `(x,y,z)`→block-type dict, BLOCK_TYPES registry, DEFAULT_HOTBAR, ground_layer/can_enter/cell_of/DEFAULT_MAX_STEP_UP), `renderer.py` (march_ray, cast_ray, pick_block, pick_voxel, screen_ray, eye_z_for, clamp_pitch, horizon_for, render_block_world_view, DEFAULT_EYE_HEIGHT=1.5, MAX_PITCH_DEGREES=70), `handlers.py` (PluginExecutor with all action handlers), `actions.py` (PLUGIN_ACTIONS schemas), `hud.py` (build_block_world_hud_commands, pygame-free).
> - **Action parameter expression parsing gotchas** (critical, caused 2 real bugs this session): `ActionExecutor._parse_value` treats `/` as division (breaks file paths — must read plain params like `wall_color` does, not via `_parse_value`), and only recognizes `random|irandom|choose` as function calls (no `cos`/`sin`/`getattr` support). `ActionExecutor._eval_bool_expression` (used by `if_condition`'s "expression" condition_type) uses `eval(expr, {"__builtins__": {}}, namespace)` — **builtins are stripped except explicitly whitelisted `abs`, `min`, `max`, `round`** — no `getattr`. Bare `x`/`y` and any instance `__dict__` attributes (not starting with `_`) are auto-injected into the namespace.
> - **GameMaker-style object JSON action-list model**: `test_variable`/`if_condition` conditionals; `if_condition` supports proper `then_actions`/`else_actions` lists (multiple actions each), unlike older flat "next action is the true-branch" style. `execute_code` gives `self`/`game`/`math`/`random`/full builtins in its own separate sandboxed exec (unlike `_eval_bool_expression`).
> - **Sample project file structure**: `project.json` (settings + assets.{sprites,sounds,backgrounds,objects,rooms,scripts,fonts,data}), external files (`objects/<name>.json`, `rooms/<name>.json`) are the source of truth, with project.json's embedded copies expected to be kept in sync (ideally byte-identical) — `_external_file` key on room/stub entries. **Critical: `ProjectManager.load_project(path)` takes the project DIRECTORY, not the project.json file path** (unlike `GameRunner(project_json)` which takes the file path directly).
> - **Welcome-tab / sample registration**: `widgets/welcome_tab.py`'s `SAMPLE_PROJECTS: List[Tuple[str,str]]` (relative path, English label), `SampleDocsDialog` for guides (`guide_path()` resolves `README.<lang>.md` with English fallback), `config/editions.py`'s `filter_samples_for_edition` (samples show ALL by default unless an edition's `sample_folders` whitelist excludes them — asymmetric vs tutorials). `tools/smoke_run_samples.py`'s `SAMPLES` list for the CI headless smoke harness.
> - **Export injection-marker mechanism** (needed for Units 8-9, researched but not yet implemented):
>   - **HTML5**: marker `// __PYGM_EXTENSION_JS__` in `export/HTML5/templates/engine.js`; `HTML5Exporter.__init__` in `export/HTML5/html5_exporter.py` replaces it via `_collect_extension_js()` which reads each enabled extension's `export_html5.js` from `events.plugin_loader.get_extension_directory()`. engine.js provides `registerRoomRenderer(fn)`/`_extRoomRenderers` (consulted by `renderExtensionRoom` which `GameRoom.render()` calls first) and `registerExtensionAction(name,fn)`/`_extActions` (consulted in `GameObject.executeAction`'s `default:` switch case).
>   - **Kivy**: two scene-level markers `# __PYGM_EXTENSION_SCENE_CODE__` and `# __PYGM_EXTENSION_BASE_CODE__` in `export/Kivy/kivy_exporter.py`'s templates, injected via `_inject_extension_scene_code`/`_inject_extension_base_code` (backed by shared `_collect_extension_code(symbol)` which `exec()`s each extension's `export_kivy.py` and pulls `SCENE_CODE`/`BASE_OBJECT_CODE` string constants). Action codegen uses `ACTION_CODEGEN: Dict[str, Callable[[gen, params, event_type], str]]` dict, discovered by `code_generator._extension_codegen()` and consulted from `_convert_simple_action`'s DEFAULT branch in `export/Kivy/code_generator.py`. Landmine: Kivy scene classes are `.format()` templates — injected code (post-`.format()`) needs NO brace-doubling, but code injected BEFORE `.format()` runs does.
>   - `extensions/raycast_2_5d/export_html5.js` and `export_kivy.py` are the exact templates to mirror for `extensions/block_world/export_html5.js`/`export_kivy.py` (don't yet exist).
> - **Empirical-verification discipline** (established and repeatedly validated this session): NEVER hand-derive geometry/math for tests or game design — always verify against the real function/handler output via a quick script before writing assertions or committing to a design. This caught: the `cell_of` rounding-boundary miscalculation, the `eye_z=1.5` staircase-footing math, the "large dx causes tunneling" discovery, and confirmed the final sample's win-path was walkable.
> - **Session-limit discipline** (CLAUDE.md standing preference): one task ≈ one commit, full suite + smoke test each time, commit+push immediately, no multi-agent Workflows (single Agent at a time when needed).

> - **`tests/test_block_world_1_sample.py`** (NEW file) — full end-to-end smoke test mirroring `TestRaycast1SampleSmoke` from `tests/test_raycast_view.py`. Key helper:
> ```python
> def _run_with_keys(held_key, frames, extra_post=None):
>     from runtime.game_runner import GameRunner
>     runner = GameRunner(PROJECT_JSON)
>     runner.language = "en"
>     runner.show_message_dialog = lambda *a, **k: None
>     runner.show_highscore_dialog = lambda *a, **k: None
>     runner._show_name_entry_dialog = lambda *a, **k: ""
>     runner.process_pending_messages = lambda *a, **k: None
>     state = {"frames": 0}
>     class _FakeClock:
>         def tick(self, fps=0):
>             f = state["frames"] = state["frames"] + 1
>             if f == 1:
>                 pygame.event.post(pygame.event.Event(pygame.KEYDOWN, key=held_key))
>             if extra_post:
>                 extra_post(f)
>             if f >= frames:
>                 runner.running = False
>             return 0
>         def get_fps(self): return 60.0
>     real_clock = pygame.time.Clock
>     pygame.time.Clock = _FakeClock
>     try:
>         result = runner.run()
>     finally:
>         pygame.time.Clock = real_clock
>     assert result is not False
>     assert state["frames"] == frames

> ## 4. Errors and Fixes
> 
> 1. **`data_file` path misread as division** (Unit 1, earlier in session): `ae._parse_value("blocks/room1.json", instance)` treated `/` as arithmetic division, corrupting the path. Fixed by reading `data_file` as a plain string (`str(parameters.get("data_file", ""))`), matching the established `wall_color`/`floor_color` convention. No user feedback needed, self-caught via test failure.
> 
> 2. **`cell_of` rounding boundary miscalculation** (Unit 4, earlier): hand-derived test assertions were wrong; empirically verified via a Python one-liner and corrected (`cell_of(CELL-1, CELL) == 1`, not `0`; "body at rest" test needed to use top-left position not center).
> 
> 3. **Terrace-height test-assertion bug** (Unit 7, this segment): wrote `assert gen.TERRACE_Z == last_step_top + 1` but the actual correct relationship is `TERRACE_Z == last_step_top` (terrace should be level with, not one above, the final step's top, for footing-continuity). Caught by running the test (`assert 4 == (4+1)` failed), fixed the assertion and its docstring.
> 
> 4. **Large `dx` in movement caused "tunneling" through collision checks**: an early manual simulation script used `dx=40` per call (more than a full cell), producing confusing results because `move_and_collide` only checks the DESTINATION cell, not intermediate ones. Recognized this was unrealistic test design (not an engine bug), redesigned the win-path verification using realistic small `dx=4` steps matching real per-frame movement, which then worked correctly and predictably (64 steps to cross ~256px).
> 
> 5. **`_eval_bool_expression` strips builtins including `getattr`**: initial win-condition design assumed `getattr(self, 'won', False)` would work in an `if_condition` expression; discovered via reading `runtime/action_executor.py` source that `eval(expression, {"__builtins__": {}}, namespace)` only whitelists `abs`,`min`,`max`,`round`. Redesigned to initialize `won=0` explicitly in the `create` event (via `set_variable`) so it's always present in `instance.__dict__` and thus auto-injected into the eval namespace, then reference it as a bare name: `"won == 0 and abs(x - 336) < 24 and abs(y - 80) < 24"`.
> 
> 6. **`ProjectManager.load_project()` returned `False` silently** when passed the `project.json` FILE path directly (`pm.load_project('samples/block_world_1/project.json')`). Root cause: the method does `project_file = project_path / self.PROJECT_FILE` internally, expecting `project_path` to be the DIRECTORY. Fixed by passing the directory (`pm.load_project('samples/block_world_1')`), which then succeeded and returned `True` with correct objects/rooms loaded. (Note: this is DIFFERENT from `GameRunner(project_json)` which DOES take the direct file path — a real API inconsistency worth remembering but not something I "fixed" in the codebase, just adapted my test-script calls to.)
>

> 2. Key Technical Concepts:
> - **pygm2 architecture**: `runtime/game_runner.py` (GameRunner, GameRoom, GameInstance), `runtime/action_executor.py` (ActionExecutor, `_parse_value`, `ACTION_ALIASES`, auto-discovery of `execute_*_action` methods via reflection), `events/action_types.py` (ActionType/ActionParameter schemas, `ACTION_TYPES` dict, `ACTION_TYPE_ALIASES`, `get_action_type()`), `runtime/action_handlers/` (modular handlers merged into `ActionExecutor.action_handlers` only for names not already covered by executor methods).
> - **Folder extension pattern**: `extensions/block_world/` — `state.py`, `renderer.py`, `handlers.py`, `actions.py`, `hud.py`, `export_html5.js`, `export_kivy.py`, `export_data.py`. Stage-C injection markers: `__PYGM_EXTENSION_JS__` (HTML5), `__PYGM_EXTENSION_SCENE_CODE__`/`__PYGM_EXTENSION_BASE_CODE__` (Kivy).
> - **Kivy exporter internals** (`export/Kivy/kivy_exporter.py`, `export/Kivy/code_generator.py`): `.format()`-templated string generation (literal `{`/`}` need doubling EXCEPT in post-`.format()` injected extension code); `ActionCodeGenerator.process_action()`/`_convert_simple_action()`; critical landmine discovered this session — `process_action`'s multi-line-code handling calls `line.strip()` on every line of a returned multi-line codegen string before re-indenting at a SINGLE shared level, so nested `if:`/`else:` blocks silently lose their indentation (must use flat ternary expressions instead, or call `self.add_line()`/`self._open_guard()` directly).
> - **HTML5 exporter internals** (`export/HTML5/html5_exporter.py`, `export/HTML5/templates/engine.js`): `registerRoomRenderer`, `registerExtensionAction`, `_extRoomRenderers`, `_extActions`, `parseNumParam`, `gmExpressionValue`, `renderDrawCommands`.
> - **Generic extension-data hook**: `_collect_extension_data()` in both `html5_exporter.py` and `kivy_exporter.py` — calls each enabled extension's optional `export_data.py`'s `collect_export_data(project_path, project_data)`, merging results into `project_data['_extension_data']`. Critical gotcha: both exporters `exec()` the extension's `export_data.py` in a BARE namespace dict (`ns = {}`) which does NOT auto-populate `__file__` (unlike a real import) and does NOT support relative imports (`from .state import` fails with `"'__name__' not in globals"`) — fixed by seeding `ns = {"__file__": str(data_file)}` and using absolute imports.
> - **Block World voxel renderer** (`extensions/block_world/renderer.py`): `march_ray` (DDA), `pick_voxel`, `_draw_wall_strip` (Tier 4a: per-column texture-cropped wall strips), `_draw_horizontal_face_textured` (Tier 4b: per-column inverse-projection texel sampling for top/bottom faces, formula `k = (eye_z - plane_z) * screen_h * cell_size`, `denom = y+0.5-horizon`, `ray_dist = (k/denom)/cos_off`, world point `gx,gy = (cam + dir*ray_dist)/cell_size`).
> - **GM-y-down vs Kivy-y-up coordinate handling**: block_world's Kivy port runs the ENTIRE DDA/projection pipeline in GM y-down space (via `_bw_gm_xy` helper), flipping ONLY at final draw calls (`_bw_fill_span`: `kivy_y = H - gm_y`). For TEXTURED draws, the v-coordinate itself also needs the flip (derived and validated via boundary tests in Tier 4a: `v=1` at Kivy-rect-TOP ↔ texel row 0 ↔ GM-top).
> - **Kivy `Texture.get_region(x, y, w, h)`**: bottom-left/GL origin — reading a specific texel row requires `th - 1 - ty` flip (established in Tier 4a's `_bw_fill_span_textured`, reused in Tier 4b's `_bw_draw_horizontal_face_textured`). Tier 4b deliberately AVOIDED `Texture.create()`/`blit_buffer()` (ambiguous row-order convention) in favor of per-sample single-pixel `get_region()` calls (no orientation ambiguity).
> - **Translation infrastructure**: `translations/pygm2_<lang>.ts` (monolithic for pt/ja/zh/es/fr; split `_core`/`_editors`/etc for de/it/ru/sl/uk), Qt context resolution from the RUNTIME CLASS name (not call-site text), `scripts/compile_translations.py`, live `QTranslator` verification pattern.
> - **Audit-is-a-lead discipline**: every claim in a planning doc must be re-verified against actual code before acting — this session found the "26 of 48 WelcomeTab messages" claim was a miscount (real gap was much smaller but revealed a DIFFERENT real bug: a whole missing `SampleDocsDialog` context).

> - **`utils/project_file_merge.py`**: single-sourced merge kernel (`merge_room_file`, `merge_object_file`, `merge_sprite_file`) used identically by `core/project_manager.py`, `runtime/game_runner.py`, and (partially) exporters — the "single point of truth" for which keys get copied from a `<type>/<name>.json` side file.

Everything actionable without further input is now done — Tiers 1–7c are all closed (7a/7c just landed), 7d/7e are explicitly marked "needs its own future planning session, do not start from this doc," and 7b is the one item still blocked on a design decision the plan itself says to get from you.

Here's a detailed breakdown of each option:

## 1. Un-placeable-on

**Mechanic:** A protected block type can't be broken (same as `is_breakable=False` today) *and* the empty cells directly touching its faces (typically just "on top of") refuse `place_block`.

**How it'd plug in:** `state.py` gets a `protected` flag alongside `breakable`. `place_block`'s target-cell resolution (currently just "is this cell air") would need one more check: does the cell being placed into sit directly against a protected block? That means looking at the block below/adjacent to the placement target, not just the target cell itself.

**What it's good for:** Keeping a spawn platform, a puzzle's floor, or a boundary wall from being buried or built over — protection reads as "this surface stays exactly as designed," which is a *level-design* concern, not a gameplay-progression one.

**Complexity:** Low — it's a second boolean next to `breakable`, checked in one more place (`place_block`). No new state per-instance, no interaction with inventory.

**Rough edge:** "Directly against" needs a precise definition — top face only, or all six faces? Top-only is simpler and covers the "don't build over my platform" case; all-faces is more restrictive and would also stop building *beside* a protected wall, which might be surprising.

## 2. Requires a specific tool/item

**Mechanic:** A protected block type can only be broken if the breaking instance's `block_inventory` (Tier 7c) contains at least one of some designated "key" block type. Breaking still consumes nothing extra by default — it's a gate check, not a trade.

**How it'd plug in:** Each protected block type would need to name its required key (e.g. a dict `{"required_key": "diamond_block"}` in `BLOCK_TYPES`), and `break_block` would check `instance.block_inventory.get(required_key, 0) > 0` before allowing removal. Since it *depends on Tier 7c's inventory*, it's a no-op world for anyone with `inventory=False` — meaning protected blocks become simply unbreakable in a creative-mode game, which is a real coupling between two originally-independent features worth being explicit about.

**What it's good for:** Progression gating — "you need a pickaxe/key to get past this," a genuine unlock mechanic. Closer to a light puzzle/adventure design than pure building.

**Complexity:** Medium — introduces a real dependency from the "protection" feature onto the "inventory" feature, plus a design question of whether possessing the key *consumes* it or is just a checked precondition (I'd default to non-consuming, since consuming would make it a one-time key rather than a tool).

## 3. Per-player permission list

**Mechanic:** A block records *who* is allowed to break/place near it — e.g., only instances matching a stored "owner" attribute, or instances flagged as "admin."

**How it'd plug in:** This is the odd one out: it's not a property of the *block type* (`BLOCK_TYPES`) at all, but of the *individual placed block* or of the *player instance*. It would need either per-block metadata (the sparse world dict today stores just a type string per cell — this would need to become a richer value, e.g. `{"type": "brick", "owner": "player_1"}`, a real storage-shape change) or a simpler global "admin flag on the instance" check or ("only the instance that placed a block can break it").

**What it's good for:** Multiplayer-flavored building (protecting your own build from griefing) — genuinely the *odd one out* for a currently single-player-shaped engine (this repo's whole architecture doesn't have a multiplayer session concept yet, per the memory note about the stashed LAN multiplayer work being deferred to its own future extension).

**Complexity:** Highest — touches the world storage format itself (block dict values would need to grow beyond a bare type string), which has ripple effects into `to_block_list`/`load_block_list` (the JSON save/load shape), the renderer's per-cell lookups, and every place that currently assumes `get_block()` returns a plain string.

---

**My read:** #1 (un-placeable-on) is the cleanest fit for what "block protection" naturally means in a single-player building sandbox, and the cheapest to build well. #2 is a legitimate and more interesting *gameplay* mechanic if there's an actual adventure/puzzle sample in mind. #3 only makes sense once there's a multiplayer story, which this codebase doesn't have yet — I'd lean against it unless multiplayer is specifically on your near-term roadmap.

---

## [2026-08-15] User

> if I choose option n°3, can I incorporate option n°2?

### Claude

Yes — they're not mutually exclusive, since they gate on different axes: #2 checks *what you're carrying* (a tool/key), #3 checks *who you are* (identity/ownership). Combined, the rule becomes something like: "only the block's owner (or an admin) can break it, and even then only with the right tool."

**What combining them actually requires, mechanically:**

The real cost is that #3 alone already forces a change #2 alone doesn't need: **the world storage format has to grow.** Right now every placed block is just a bare type string (`blocks["x,y,z"] = "brick"`). Ownership needs a per-*placed-block* owner, not a per-*block-type* property, so each entry would become something like `{"type": "brick", "owner": "player_1"}`. That change ripples into:

- `get_block`/`set_block`/`remove_block` — need to read/write the richer shape
- `iter_blocks` / `column_index` — the renderer's hot-path cache currently stores `(z, block_type)` tuples and hands them straight to `block_face_textures()`; that unpacking has to change everywhere it's used
- `to_block_list`/`load_block_list` — the on-disk JSON shape (and thus every existing saved world file) needs an `owner` field added
- A design call for **pre-authored terrain**: blocks loaded via `load_block_world` have no natural "owner" — they'd default to `owner=None`, meaning either "unprotected" (anyone can break world terrain, only *player-placed* blocks are ever protected) or you need a separate "admin territory" concept for map-authored protection. That's a real decision, not a detail.

Once that storage change exists, layering #2 on top is cheap: the block-*type* registry (`BLOCK_TYPES`) already doesn't need to change for a required-key check, so `break_block` just becomes two stacked conditions — "are you the owner/admin?" AND "do you have the key?" — rather than one.

**Net effect:** combining them doesn't save work — it's roughly the *sum* of both, dominated by #3's storage-format migration (the biggest single cost of any of the four original options). It would also need re-sizing: the plan doc originally estimated 7b as "small," and this combination is meaningfully bigger than that, closer to a medium unit on its own.

One more thing worth naming honestly: #3's real value (protecting *your* build from *other players*) only pays off once there's an actual multiplayer session — and this engine doesn't have one yet (the LAN multiplayer work is stashed, deferred to its own future extension per my notes). Single-player, an "owner" check just becomes "was this placed by the player" vs "was this in the original map," which is a real distinction but a narrower one than true per-player permissions.

---

## [2026-08-15] User

> If we do option n°3 only after we introduce a multiplayer option on a school network, would that be more complicated than if we do it now?

### Claude

Waiting would be *simpler* overall, not more complicated — for a specific reason: the piece of #3 that's hard (a real "who is this player" identity) doesn't exist yet, and building it now means guessing.

**Doing it now (single-player, no multiplayer):**
- There's only ever one real player instance in any room today, so "owner" wouldn't have a natural identity to hang off — you'd have to invent a placeholder scheme (instance name? a generated player-id field?) with no second player to ever test it against.
- The storage-format change (block entries becoming `{"type":, "owner":}` instead of a bare string) still has to happen, but you'd be committing to *your own guess* at what "player identity" looks like.
- When multiplayer eventually arrives — per my notes, decided to be built as its own folder extension, not a core change — it will need to define a real identity/session model (client id, player slot, whatever it turns out to be). If that doesn't match the placeholder you guessed now, you're looking at a second migration: reworking the ownership field, and potentially converting any world files that were saved with the old scheme.
- And in the meantime, the feature is genuinely inert — "protect this from other players" has no other players to protect it from, so it's schema and code weight with no payoff until multiplayer lands.

**Doing it after multiplayer exists:**
- Ownership hooks into whatever real identity/session concept multiplayer actually settles on, instead of a guess.
- The storage-format migration happens exactly once, timed to when it's actually useful and testable.
- You'd likely also inherit any access-control conventions multiplayer already has to establish anyway (e.g., how it identifies "which client placed this"), rather than inventing a parallel one.

The one cost that's identical either way is the storage-format change itself — turning block entries from bare strings into `{"type":, "owner":}` dicts, and updating every place that touches that shape (`get_block`/`set_block`/`iter_blocks`/`column_index`/the renderer's texture lookups/`to_block_list`/`load_block_list`). That work doesn't get cheaper by front-loading it; it just risks being done twice.

Worth noting: this timing question only affects #3. Options #1 (un-placeable-on) and #2 (tool/key-gated) don't depend on player identity at all — they're buildable now regardless of what you decide about #3 and multiplayer timing.

> - **Folder extension pattern**: `extensions/<name>/` with `extension.json` manifest (`name`, `version`, `author`, `description`, `enabled`, `provides_actions`), `__init__.py` exposing `PLUGIN_ACTIONS`/`PluginExecutor`/optionally `PLUGIN_ROOM_RENDERERS`/now also `PLUGIN_FRAME_UPDATES`. > - **`events/plugin_loader.py`**: `PluginLoader` class with `_load_room_renderers(renderers)` (mirrors: iterate, call `register_room_renderer`, return count) and **NEW: `_load_frame_updates(updates)`** (mirrors: iterate `(func, phase)` pairs, call `register_frame_update(func, phase)`, return count). > - **Wire protocol** (my new design, following the plan's own recommendations): TCP (not UDP — plan's explicit recommendation, kept), JSON lines newline-delimited, message shape `{"t": "snap", "i": [[sync_id, x, y, rotation, image_index, visible], ...]}`.
> - **Sync ID model**: deterministic, assigned by enumeration index over `room.instances` at network-init time — both host and client load the identical project, so indices match without any negotiation (matches the stash's own model exactly).

> - **CLI/env-var design** (Phase 2, not yet implemented but designed and referenced in handlers.py already): `run_game.py` sets `PYGM_NET_MODE`/`PYGM_NET_HOST_ADDR`/`PYGM_NET_PORT` env vars from `--net-host`/`--net-client HOST`/`--net-port PORT` CLI flags, WITHOUT importing the extension (keeps the generic bootstrap script extension-agnostic); the extension's own frame-update hooks check these env vars as a fallback when no `set_network_mode` action was ever authored, so CLI-only testing works even in a project with zero multiplayer UI.

>   - `class NetworkHost`: `__init__(self, port=DEFAULT_PORT)`; `start()` (binds `("", port)`, `SO_REUSEADDR`, `listen(8)`, `setblocking(False)`); `_accept_pending()` (loops `self._listen_sock.accept()` catching `BlockingIOError`/`OSError` to stop, sets accepted conn `setblocking(True)` + `settimeout(0.2)`, appends to `self._clients`); `broadcast_snapshot(rows)` (calls `_accept_pending()` first, encodes once, `sendall()` to each client, drops failed ones via `OSError` catch, rebuilds `self._clients` from survivors); `close()` (closes all clients + listen socket).
>   - `class NetworkClient`: `__init__(self, host, port=DEFAULT_PORT)`; `connect()` (creates socket, `settimeout(5.0)`, `connect((host, port))`, then `setblocking(False)`); `poll()` (loops `recv(65536)` catching `BlockingIOError` to stop / `OSError` sets `self._sock = None`; buffers in `self._buffer`; if no `b"\n"` present returns `None`; otherwise splits on newline, keeps last partial segment as new buffer, returns `json.loads()` of the LAST complete line — i.e., "latest snapshot wins" semantics, older buffered messages in the same poll are discarded); `close()`.

## [2026-08-15] User

> # Autonomous loop check
> 
> You're being invoked on a timer while the user is away or occupied. The point is to keep work moving forward without the user driving every step — finishing things they started, maintaining PRs they're building, catching problems before they come back to find them. You're a steward, not an initiator. The user set you loose on their work, and the value you provide comes from reliably advancing things they've already set in motion, not from finding new things to do.
> 
> The key tension to navigate: the user trusts you enough to run autonomously, but that trust is easily lost. Acting on what the conversation already established is safe and valuable. Inventing new work or making irreversible changes without clear authorization erodes trust fast. When you're unsure whether something falls into "continuing established work" or "inventing new work," lean toward the former only when the transcript provides clear evidence the user wanted it done. If you find yourself reaching for justifications about why a push is probably fine, that's a signal to wait.
> 
> ## What to act on
> 
> The current conversation is your highest-signal source — re-read the transcript above, since everything there is something the user was actively engaged with. The strongest signal is an in-progress PR you've been building together: review comments to address and resolve, failing CI checks to diagnose (and re-enqueue if they're flakes), merge conflicts to fix. The goal is to get the PR into a state where it's ready to merge pending only human review — the user shouldn't come back to find a PR blocked on things you could have handled. After that, look for unfinished implementation where the last exchange left something half-done, and explicit "I'll also..." or "next I'll..." commitments the conversation made and didn't honor. Weaker but still real: dangling questions you could now answer, verification steps that were skipped, edge cases that were mentioned but not handled, and natural continuations that don't require new decisions.
> 
> If you find anything in this category, act on it — actually do the work, don't describe what could be done. Run the tests, don't say "you could run the tests." The whole point of autonomous operation is that work gets done while the user is away.
> 
> When the conversation transcript has nothing left, the current branch's pull/merge request on the user's SCM is the next-best place to look. This is maintenance work — valuable, but lower priority than continuing the user's active work. Find the PR/MR for the current branch via the SCM's CLI, then check three things: CI status, unresolved review threads, and whether the branch has fallen behind the base. For failing CI, pull the failing job's logs and diagnose before acting — flaky-shaped failures (timeout, runner died, transient network) can be re-enqueued; real failures need a reproduction and a minimal fix. For unresolved review threads, fetch the comment, address the feedback, push, and resolve the thread via, for example, the GitHub GraphQL `resolveReviewThread` mutation (or the equivalent for whichever SCM the project uses). Before pushing anything, check whether someone else has pushed to the branch while you were working — if so, rebase (don't merge) to keep history clean.
> 
> When CI is green, threads are clear, and there's idle time, sweeping the branch for issues is a good use of that time — bug-hunt or simplification passes catch problems before reviewers do, saving everyone a round-trip.
> 
> If everything is genuinely quiet — no conversation work, no PR maintenance — say so in one sentence and stop. No summary of what you checked, no list of what you might do later. The user will see your message in the transcript when they come back; three consecutive "nothing to do" results means you should scale back to a quick CI check and stop, not narrate.
> 
> ## Repeated invocations
>

| **Kivy debug/release presets** | Medium | buildozer already handles signing mechanics; the real work is an honest keystore UI with Google Play's "lose it and you can never update this app again" risk made explicit |

> 
> 2. Key Technical Concepts:
> - **pygm2 export architecture**: `export/desktop/pygame_desktop_exporter.py` (`BasePygameDesktopExporter`, the shared pipeline for exe/linux/macos, freezes real `runtime/game_runner.py` via PyInstaller), `export/exe/exe_exporter.py`, `export/linux/linux_exporter.py`, `export/macos/macos_exporter.py` (thin per-platform wrappers), `export/HTML5/html5_exporter.py` + `export/HTML5/templates/{engine.js,game_template.html}`, `export/Kivy/kivy_exporter.py` (source generation), `export/Kivy/buildspec_generator.py` (`BuildspecGenerator`, generates `buildozer.spec`), `export/android/android_exporter.py` (`AndroidExporter`, orchestrates the whole Android build via buildozer), `export/android/wsl_bridge.py` (`WSLBridge`, runs buildozer inside WSL when host is Windows).
> - **PyInstaller spec templates**: `SPEC_TEMPLATE_ONEFILE`/`SPEC_TEMPLATE_ONEDIR` in `pygame_desktop_exporter.py`, `.format()`-templated with `{extra_options}` (per-platform hook via `_spec_exe_options()`) and `{trailer}` (macOS's `BUNDLE()`).
> - **PyInstaller version-info resource**: a text file PyInstaller itself evals at build time with `VSVersionInfo`/`FixedFileInfo`/`StringFileInfo`/`StringTable`/`StringStruct`/`VarFileInfo`/`VarStruct` already in scope — no need to import `PyInstaller.utils.win32.versioninfo` to generate the text.
> - **HTML5 export's key architectural insight** (self-discovered, proved correct): `img.src = spritesData[name]` and `new Audio(soundsData[name])` in `engine.js` accept a `data:` URI or an ordinary relative URL identically — meaning external-asset mode needs ZERO engine.js changes, only different string VALUES baked into the exported page.
> - **Classic `<script>` tag scoping**: top-level `let`/`const` in one non-module `<script>` tag are visible to a LATER `<script>` tag in the same document/realm (verified reasoning, not just assumed) — this is why splitting `engine.js` into its own `<script src>` tag doesn't break its ability to read `gameData`/`spritesData`/`soundsData`/`pako` set up by an earlier inline script block.
> - **buildozer/python-for-android (p4a) release signing convention**: `P4A_RELEASE_KEYSTORE`/`P4A_RELEASE_KEYSTORE_PASSWD`/`P4A_RELEASE_KEYALIAS`/`P4A_RELEASE_KEYALIAS_PASSWD` environment variables are p4a's OWN established mechanism — pygm2 doesn't need to reinvent signing, just plumb its own settings through to these.
> - **WSL execution model constraint** (newly understood this segment): env vars must be injected as `export FOO=bar` lines INSIDE the generated bash script text that `wsl bash <script>` executes — a Python-side `subprocess.Popen(env=...)` does NOT propagate into the WSL guest, since `wsl.exe` is a separate process boundary.
> - **`buildspec_generator.py`'s f-string template convention**: `_create_buildozer_spec_content()` builds `buildozer.spec` content via an f-string (`f"""[app]\n...\n"""`), so any literal `{`/`}` needed in the output (e.g. Gradle repository syntax examples in comments) must be doubled (`{{`/`}}`) — confirmed via existing doubled-brace comment lines at 291/293.
> - **Google Play's AAB requirement**: new app submissions to Play Store require the Android App Bundle (`.aab`) format rather than a raw `.apk`; buildozer supports this via `android.release_artifact = aab` in `buildozer.spec`, only meaningful for `buildozer android release` (a debug build always produces `.apk` regardless).
> - **Keystore consequence** (load-bearing UX concern, not yet implemented): losing an Android release keystore means the app can NEVER be updated again under the same Play Store listing — any future UI must say this explicitly before generating/accepting one, matching this repo's "stop lying to users about consequences" standing preference.
> - **Testing conventions used**: real exporter objects driven through `_stage()`/`_write_spec()`/`export()` calls against real or synthetic project fixtures (not mocked file I/O) for exe/macos/HTML5; AST-based parsing (`ast.parse`, `ast.walk`, `ast.literal_eval`) to check generated Python source rather than fragile string matching; regex extraction of embedded gzip+base64 JSON blobs from generated HTML (`decompressData\("([A-Za-z0-9+/=]+)"\)` pattern) to prove real behavior rather than asserting on raw un-decoded text.

>   ```python
>   def _parse_version_tuple(version_str, parts: int = 4) -> tuple:
>       """Best-effort parse of a project version string like "1.2.3" into a
>       tuple of `parts` non-negative ints, padded with 0s. Never raises..."""
>       nums = []
>       for piece in str(version_str or "").split("."):
>           digits = "".join(ch for ch in piece if ch.isdigit())
>           nums.append(int(digits) if digits else 0)
>       nums = (nums + [0] * parts)[:parts]
>       return tuple(nums)
> 
>   def _version_info_text(app_name: str, version_str: str) -> str:
>       """A PyInstaller version-info resource file..."""
>       ver_tuple = _parse_version_tuple(version_str, 4)
>       ver_str = ".".join(str(n) for n in ver_tuple)
>       def esc(s):
>           return str(s).replace("\\", "\\\\").replace("'", "\\'")
>       return f"""# UTF-8
>   VSVersionInfo(
>     ffi=FixedFileInfo(
>       filevers={ver_tuple!r},
>       prodvers={ver_tuple!r},
>       mask=0x3f, flags=0x0, OS=0x40004, fileType=0x1, subtype=0x0, date=(0, 0)
>     ),
>     kids=[
>       StringFileInfo([StringTable(u'040904B0',
>         [StringStruct(u'FileDescription', u'{esc(app_name)}'),
>         StringStruct(u'FileVersion', u'{esc(ver_str)}'),
>         StringStruct(u'InternalName', u'{esc(app_name)}'),
>         StringStruct(u'OriginalFilename', u'{esc(app_name)}.exe'),
>         StringStruct(u'ProductName', u'{esc(app_name)}'),
>         StringStruct(u'ProductVersion', u'{esc(ver_str)}')])]),
>       VarFileInfo([VarStruct(u'Translation', [1033, 1200])])
>     ]
>   )
>   """
>   ```

> - **De-risked HTML5 external-asset loading before writing code**: grepped `engine.js` for `spritesData`/`soundsData` consumption and found `img.src = spritesData[name]` (line 3782) and `new Audio(src)` inside `acquirePooledAudio` (line 226) — both accept a `data:` URI or a relative URL transparently via the browser's own `.src=`/`Audio()` semantics. This confirmed the plan doc's own prediction ("A real simplification worth knowing before scoping this too big") and meant Phase 2 (sounds) needed zero JS changes, only Python-side changes to what string gets embedded.
> - **Verified `<script>` tag scoping safety** before splitting `engine.js` into a separate `<script src>` tag: reasoned through (not just assumed) that top-level `let`/`const` declared in one classic (non-module) `<script>` tag ARE visible to a subsequently-executed `<script>` tag in the same document/realm — standard, well-established browser behavior — so `gameData`/`spritesData`/`soundsData`/`pako` set up by the first inline script block remain readable by `engine.js` whether it's inlined in the same tag or loaded via `<script src="engine.js">` immediately after, as long as document order (not `async`/`defer`) is preserved.
> - **Confirmed WSL env-var propagation model** by reading `wsl_bridge.py`'s `run_buildozer()` in full and observing `subprocess.Popen(['wsl', 'bash', script_path], ...)` has no `env=` kwarg — concluding that any new environment variables (keystore paths/passwords) needed by a release build running inside WSL must be emitted as `export FOO=bar` lines textually inside the generated bash script (`_build_script_text`), not passed via the Python-side subprocess call. This is a not-yet-implemented finding that will shape the next edit.
> - **Scoped the "keystore UI" question deliberately** (per the plan doc's own recommendation) to build the MECHANISM only for this pass — accept `build_type`/`keystore_path`/`keystore_password`/`key_alias`/`key_password` via the existing `export_settings` dict (matching how `icon_path`/`language`/`include_debug` already flow through this whole export subsystem), deferring an actual Qt dialog for browsing/generating a keystore with the "back this up, it's permanent" warning as a natural, separately-scoped follow-up — not yet implemented, but the architecture decision is made.
> - **Identified but have NOT yet resolved**: shell-injection safety when interpolating keystore passwords into the WSL bash script text — plan is to use POSIX single-quote escaping (`'\''` trick) via a small `_bash_single_quote()` helper, not yet written.

> 
> # Artifact runtime capabilities
> 
> A published Artifact page can declare **runtime capabilities** — abilities the claude.ai viewer grants the page at open time — by passing `capabilities: {name: config}` to the Artifact tool. The control plane is the authority on valid names and config shapes. Declaration gestures: **omitting** `capabilities` on a redeploy carries the stored declaration forward unchanged (and preserves the artifact's stored contract pin); an **empty object** `{}` is the explicit clear-all; a **non-empty object** is a full-set declaration (anything stored but not restated is revoked). Moving a republished artifact's runtime version is a deliberate gesture — pass `contract: 'latest'` to upgrade, or a specific version to pin or roll back — never a side effect of editing.
> 
> **Available capabilities:** `artifact`, `downloads`, `mcp`, `self` — the complete set of capability names you may declare. Anything not listed is unavailable to this user.
> 
> Runtime contract 0.2.11
> 
> 
> Capability namespaces live behind `claude.use(name)`: `const db = await claude.use("db")` resolves the capability's namespace once this view can run its code, or `null` when it cannot — not served here, not granted at initialization, or failed to load, indistinguishable by design. Branch on `null` and design for absence; never probe `window.claude` members. Permission stays on the calls themselves: a consent prompt, rate limit, or policy refusal arrives on the first call, never from `use()`. For capabilities this view serves, the promise is memoized — awaiting `use("db")` again is free; an unknown name resolves `null`. Works for every capability this contract serves; each capability's own section and type definitions document its namespace.
> 
> 
> --- capability: artifact ---
> 
> Use `artifact` for pages that should remember what people do with them: polls, sign-up sheets, checklists, trackers, boards — the page is the record. Declare `capabilities: {artifact: {}}`; owner and editors write as themselves; read-only viewers reject `not_granted`/`not_writer` — show a read-only view. On a LIVE DOC the page's markup IS the shared document: whatever a viewer's click or keystroke changes in the DOM (text, attributes, elements, checkbox/input state) is saved as them and reaches every view and Claude; what script does with no gesture (load, timers, fetch) is not. Serve content as HTML (what script renders on load is not the document), change it freely in handlers, and keep per-viewer UI (filters, tabs, sort order, drafts) inside `<artifact-local>` or on `data-local-*` attributes. On a classic artifact `const artifact = await claude.use("artifact")`, then `artifact.publish(html)` saves a new version; `conflict` is routine: keep the source a render function of state, never the live DOM.
> 
> 
> --- capability: downloads ---
> 
> The `downloads` capability lets a published page offer a generated file to the viewer: declare `capabilities: {downloads: true}`, then `const downloads = await claude.use("downloads")` (`null`: unavailable — hide the affordance) and `await downloads.save({filename, data})`. The viewer sees a confirmation and may decline — a save is never silent or guaranteed, so offer it on explicit viewer intent and handle rejection. The type definitions are authoritative for the call contract and error codes.
> 
> 
> --- capability: mcp ---
> 
> `mcp` lets a published page call the viewer's claude.ai connectors: `const mcp = await claude.use("mcp")` (`null`: unavailable); calls run with the viewer's credentials, never exposing tokens. Declare `capabilities: {mcp: {servers: [{server, tools}]}}` — `server` is a connector's display name. Keep the manifest minimal: it is a viewer-consented grant; a declaring page cannot be shared publicly. Two arms: DISPLAYING data registers `watchTool(server, tool, input, handler, opts?)` — replays cache, refreshes when stale, polls only via `refetchInterval`, returns a sync unsubscribe; an ACTION calls `callTool` once and reads `result.payload`. Tool failures REJECT (`tool_error`); watch failures arrive as handler error events. Type definitions first: branch UX per error code, retry only `retryable` errors, drop data on authz denials, drive freshness UI from `result.cache.storedAt`. They omit argument names and result encoding: observe a real request/response pair per tool, or say so at publish — never guess.
> 
> 
> --- capability: self ---
> 
> `self` is the former name of the `artifact` capability (renamed). It remains for compatibility: published pages and previously generated code that declare `capabilities: {self: {}}`, feature-check `window.claude.self`, or call `claude.use("self")` keep working unchanged — both names resolve this same capability. Do not use it in new pages: declare `capabilities: {artifact: {}}` and obtain the namespace with `await claude.use("artifact")`; see the artifact section for how to use it.
> 
> 
> **Your connectors this session.** None are connected right now — they may still be connecting, or the user has none. Look for tools prefixed `mcp__claude_ai_*` in your tool list; each is named `mcp__claude_ai_<connector>__<tool>`. Only claude.ai connectors are valid — locally-configured MCP servers are not. The manifest's `tools` array takes the connector's upstream tool names (as returned by `listTools()` / `/v1/mcp_servers`), which can differ from the normalized `<toolName>` segment when an upstream name contains `.` or spaces. In hermetic/CI sessions where connectors aren't loaded but `$CLAUDE_CODE_OAUTH_TOKEN` is set, fetch the list via Bash: `curl -H 'anthropic-version: 2023-06-01' -H 'anthropic-beta: mcp-servers-2025-12-04' -H "Authorization: Bearer $CLAUDE_CODE_OAUTH_TOKEN" https://api.anthropic.com/v1/mcp_servers?limit=1000`; in that case use each entry's `display_name` as the `server` value (exact display names are always accepted alongside tool-prefix segments).
> 
> **Call contract** (runtime contract 0.2.11). The platform-served `window.claude` type definitions for this contract are extracted under `/tmp/claude-1000/bundled-skills/2.1.235/a5bcd3eb532f9d90f21859f399ff519f/artifact-capabilities`: `0.2.11/artifact.d.ts`, `0.2.11/claude.d.ts`, `0.2.11/downloads.d.ts`, `0.2.11/mcp.d.ts`, `0.2.11/self.d.ts`. Read `/tmp/claude-1000/bundled-skills/2.1.235/a5bcd3eb532f9d90f21859f399ff519f/artifact-capabilities/0.2.11/claude.d.ts` (how a page reaches any capability on this contract) and `/tmp/claude-1000/bundled-skills/2.1.235/a5bcd3eb532f9d90f21859f399ff519f/artifact-capabilities/0.2.11/mcp.d.ts` before writing any code that calls the `mcp` capability — they are authoritative for this contract version over any remembered API shape. The type definitions cover only the call envelope — they do not tell you a connector tool's argument names or its result encoding. Never publish a page that calls a connector tool without having observed one real request/response pair for that tool in this session; if you cannot safely observe one (for example, the connector is unauthenticated here, or calling the tool would have side effects), say that explicitly to the user at publish time — in your reply, not as a note inside the published page — instead of shipping a guessed shape. Observed response payloads are the user's real data: learn the shape from them, but never embed the observed values in the published page as sample or placeholder data.

- `create_instance` turned out to be unimplemented in the HTML5 engine (only `create_moving_instance` exists) — worked around in the game, didn't fix the engine (didn't want to scope-creep further).
- Match-3's score had to route through the synced `game.score` rather than a custom Python attribute, since HTML5's execute_code runs in an isolated proxy that only syncs back a handful of fields.

**One real limitation, by design:** Match-3 needs Python (Pyodide, loaded from a CDN) — this works fine as a normal hosted or double-clicked file, but won't work if you ever paste this into a sandboxed viewer that blocks external network requests (like Claude's own Artifacts), so I didn't try to publish it that way. Host it normally (your own site, or literally emailing/handing over the file) and everything works, including Match-3.

## [2026-08-21] User

> How does the entry room work, how were the objects placed and programmed?

### Claude

Now I have everything to give you the full picture.

## The room itself

`rm_hub` is a 480×480 room with `background_image` set to a `bg_hub` background asset — a soft gradient-sky-with-clouds PNG I generated procedurally (not hand-drawn), covering the whole canvas so the buttons sit on something nicer than a flat color.

## How the objects are "placed"

Here's the part that's a little unusual: **every instance in the room sits at (0, 0)**. Look at `rooms/rm_hub.json` — title and all six buttons, all `x: 0, y: 0`.

That's deliberate, not an oversight. Room instances only carry `x`, `y`, `rotation`, `scale`, `visible` — there's no per-instance "custom variable" field in this engine, so I couldn't place one generic "button" object six times with a different position/target room baked into each placement. Instead, **each category got its own dedicated object type** (`obj_btn_maze`, `obj_btn_plateforme`, etc.), and each object hardcodes its own screen position and destination directly in its own code. The room doesn't position them at all — the objects position *themselves* when they draw.

## How each button is programmed

Every button object has exactly two events:

**`draw`** — six `draw_rectangle`/`draw_sprite`/`draw_text` calls: the colored card background, its border, the category icon (`draw_sprite`, offset by half the icon's size since sprite-drawing uses the top-left corner, not a center origin), and the label text. All coordinates are literal numbers — `x1: 33, y1: 65, x2: 233, y2: 189` for the maze card, for instance.

**`mouse_left_press`** — a bounding-box hit-test, built from **four chained `test_variable` checks** (`mouse_x >= 33`, `mouse_x <= 233`, `mouse_y >= 65`, `mouse_y <= 189`) followed by `goto_room`. `mouse_x`/`mouse_y` get set on the instance automatically right before this event fires, in *room* coordinates — every instance with a `mouse_left_press` handler gets the event on every click, with no automatic position gating, so each object has to check for itself whether the click actually landed on it.

The four-check chain relies on GameMaker-style "skip-next" semantics: each `test_variable` that comes back false skips the next single action, and — this is the detail that actually matters — a skipped *question* action skips its own guarded action too, recursively. So if any one of the four checks fails, everything downstream including `goto_room` gets skipped as a unit; only a click where all four are true reaches the room change. (This is also literally why the game didn't work the first time I exported it to HTML5 — the web engine had that recursive-skip behavior missing, so multiple buttons would fire off one click. I found and fixed that bug in the engine itself, pushed to `main`.)

The title object is simpler: just two `draw_text` calls, no interaction — it doesn't need a click handler since it isn't clickable.

**Quit/abandon button** (used in every *other* room, not the hub) works identically — same four-check hit-test pattern, just against a fixed top-left rectangle, `goto_room("rm_hub")` at the end.

## Room editor: click-to-cycle through stacked objects

I found the real spot — `find_instance_at` in `editors/room_editor/room_canvas.py` always picked the topmost instance by list order, with nothing underneath ever reachable. Since a room instance has no per-instance custom-variable slot (which is *why* my hub's 7 objects all sit at (0,0), each with its own dedicated object type instead), that's exactly the situation your project produces.

Fixed: a repeated click at the same spot, with the previous pick still selected, now advances to the next instance underneath — the standard "click again to cycle" convention. The status bar names it explicitly: *"3 objects stacked here, click again to cycle (2/3)"*. Clicking anywhere else resets to the topmost, same as before. 7 regression tests, full suite green (3764 passed), committed and pushed to `main`.

- **Platform**: blocks now have a real grass-green cap over the brown body (matching the actual in-game tile), and the character's feet land exactly on the grass line of the elevated block instead of floating beside it.
- **Maze**: this is now a genuine maze — a recursive-backtracker algorithm carves real passages through a 5×5 grid, walls drawn only where no passage exists, with a blue start dot and green exit dot marking a real route through it.
- **Raycast**: rebuilt as an actual first-person corridor — converging left/right walls with perspective mortar lines, a sky-colored ceiling, a dark floor, and a bright doorway glow at the vanishing point, instead of the old abstract triangle.

## [2026-08-21] User

> While playing the blockworld level, the UP key shiftzs the view towards the left without turning, DOWN key shifts the view to the right, LEFT key moves backwars and RIGHT key moves forward

### Claude

Let's export and verify via a real browser that UP now moves the player forward toward the goal.

Excellent — the staircase is now dead ahead, confirming UP walks straight toward it. Let's verify DOWN/LEFT/RIGHT too.

Let's do one final full navigation sweep, then copy the fixed files to the deliverable location.

Everything still works, and the block-world intro message now correctly says "avancer/reculer" (forward/backward) and "te déplacer sur le côté" (move sideways). Let's copy the final files.

Fixed. Here's what was actually going on:

**The bug:** Block-world movement is compass-locked (fixed world directions), not relative to which way you're looking — the underlying `move_and_collide` action only takes literal x/y deltas, no angle, so true "walk toward wherever you're facing" movement isn't available without pulling in Python (which I deliberately avoided for this level). I had Up/Down driving the world's Y-axis and Left/Right driving X — but the camera's default facing direction (and the staircase itself) both sit along the X-axis, so Left/Right happened to look like forward/back, and Up/Down were pure sideways strafing.

**The fix:** swapped which arrow keys drive which axis — Up/Down now drive X (forward/backward, and conveniently the exact direction the staircase runs), Left/Right drive Y (sideways strafe). Verified with a real keypress in a live browser: holding Up moves the player from x=128 to x=272 with y completely unchanged, and the screenshot shows the staircase dead-center, closer than before — genuinely walking straight toward the goal. Also corrected the level's own instructions text, which had claimed Left/Right "turns" (it doesn't — there's no true turning in this simplified scheme, just strafing).

Re-verified the whole game end-to-end again afterward (all six levels, zero console errors), and updated both the project and the exported HTML file in `~/Dropbox/PyGameMaker Projects/Jeu Promo/`.

> 2. Key Technical Concepts:
> 
> - **pygm2 Room Editor instance model**: `editors/room_editor/room_canvas.py`'s `RoomCanvas` holds `self.instances` (plain list, paint order = list order, later = on top) and `self.selected_instances`. Room JSON instances carry only `object_name, x, y, rotation, scale_x, scale_y, visible` — no per-instance custom-variable slot, so distinguishing otherwise-identical placements (as in the hub, all at (0,0)) requires giving each a unique object type.
> - **Click-to-cycle selection pattern**: `find_instances_at(pos)` returns the whole topmost-first "pile" of instances under a click point; `find_instance_at(pos)` becomes a thin wrapper returning `pile[0]`; `RoomCanvas.last_click_pile` tracks state so a repeated click at the same spot (with the same instance still the sole selection) advances to the next instance underneath, wrapping around; any other click resets to topmost.
> - **GameMaker-style recursive skip-chain conditional semantics**: (background from earlier in the session, referenced again) a skipped QUESTION action (e.g. `test_variable`) takes its own guarded unit down with it recursively — both `runtime/action_executor.py`'s `_execute_action_list_inner` (desktop, `_QUESTION_ACTIONS` frozenset) and (from earlier in this session, not this segment) `engine.js`'s `_executeActionsInner` implement this.
> - **Procedural PIL-based sprite generation**: `gen_sprites.py` in the scratchpad builds all custom game icons/sprites via `PIL.Image`/`ImageDraw`, no external art. Recursive-backtracker maze generation algorithm (fixed-seed `random.Random(7)` for reproducibility) used to build a genuine small maze icon.
> - **Block-world extension movement/camera math**: `extensions/block_world/renderer.py` — `facing_screen_rad = math.radians(-camera.facing_angle)`, then `dir_x, dir_y = math.cos(facing_screen_rad), math.sin(facing_screen_rad)`. At default `facing_angle=0`, camera looks toward world +X. `move_and_collide(dx, dy)` (from `extensions/block_world/actions.py`) takes literal world-space pixel deltas, NOT an angle — no facing-relative movement is possible without trigonometry, which isn't expressible in the plain action-parameter expression evaluator (no cos/sin support, unlike `random`/`irandom`/`choose`) without resorting to `execute_code` (Python/Pyodide), which was deliberately avoided for this level to keep it Pyodide-free.
> - **GameMaker-style movement-blocking via `solid` + collision-event-existence rule** (matches GameMaker 7.0, ported identically across desktop/Kivy/HTML5): a moving instance's proposed move is blocked if (1) a `collision_with_<other>` event is registered between the two object types in EITHER direction (even with empty `actions: []` — existence alone counts), AND (2) at least one of the two objects has `solid: true`. Two non-solid objects never block, only fire collision events via post-movement overlap.
> - **The "already overlapping → let it escape" rule** (present identically in `runtime/game_runner.py`'s `check_movement_collision_with_blocker` AND `export/HTML5/templates/engine.js`'s `_movementBlocker`): if the mover's CURRENT position already overlaps the blocker, blocking is skipped entirely for that move (to avoid a permanent oscillation-freeze), but this is a **per-frame check that re-evaluates fresh each call** — the mechanism doesn't prevent MULTIPLE consecutive moves from exploiting this once overlap begins, potentially letting an instance walk freely through a solid object once ANY initial overlap occurs (until it stops overlapping, at which point blocking resumes normally against subsequent motion, potentially trapping the instance on the far side).
> - **Sprite `bbox_left/bbox_top/bbox_right/bbox_bottom` fields**: an optional, narrower collision box distinct from the sprite's full width/height (e.g., `samples/raycast_1/sprites/spr_person.json` has an explicit 8x8 box centered in a 16x16 sprite: `bbox_left=4, bbox_top=4, bbox_right=12, bbox_bottom=12`). **Desktop's `check_movement_collision_with_blocker` explicitly uses these bbox_* fields** to compute the actual collision rectangle (`bw1 = s1.bbox_right - s1.bbox_left`, offset by `s1.bbox_left/top` from the sprite's origin-adjusted position). Wall sprites (`spr_wall_h`, `spr_wall_v`) have no explicit bbox_* fields in their JSON (only width/height/origin_x/origin_y), implying a full-sprite default on desktop.
> - **HTML5's `getBoundingBox()`/`boxWidth()`/`boxHeight()`** (`export/HTML5/templates/engine.js` ~line 3109-3126) use only `spriteInfo.width`/`spriteInfo.height` (the FULL per-frame sprite size) and origin offset — **they do NOT reference bbox_left/top/right/bottom at all**, a confirmed discrepancy from desktop's narrower, inset collision box. This was the finding I had JUST made when the summary was requested, not yet fully verified as the root cause or acted upon.
> - **`makeSpriteInfo(spriteName)`** (`engine.js` line 3771) — the function that builds each instance's `spriteInfo` object from the exported sprite data; I had NOT yet read its body to confirm whether `bbox_left/top/right/bottom` fields are even present in the data it receives (i.e., whether the exporter embeds them at all, or whether this needs new plumbing on the Python export side too, not just the JS collision-box computation).
> - **HTML5 export architecture reminders** (from earlier in the session, still relevant): `export/HTML5/html5_exporter.py`'s `encode_sprites` embeds sprite metadata as base64 JSON; `export/HTML5/templates/engine.js` is the shared runtime template, string-substituted at export time; extensions (raycast_2_5d, block_world) contribute their own `export_html5.js` fragments concatenated at a marker.
> - **Deliverable structure**: `~/Dropbox/PyGameMaker Projects/Jeu Promo/` = live pygm2 project (rooms/, objects/, sprites/, backgrounds/, project.json — the SOURCE OF TRUTH once the user starts hand-editing via their own IDE); `~/Dropbox/PyGameMaker Projects/Jeu Promo/export_web/Jeu Promo - Cours de Programmation.html` = the finished, patched, playable export. **Critical constraint discovered this segment**: once the user edits the project directly via their IDE, my old scratchpad `build.py` script (which regenerates the whole project from source samples) must NOT be re-run, as it would silently overwrite/discard the user's hand-edits. All exports from this point forward must be done directly from the live Dropbox project folder.
> - **`patch_html.py`** (in scratchpad): post-export patch script injecting responsive CSS, a touch D-pad + action button (dispatching real `KeyboardEvent`s matching engine.js's own `window.addEventListener('keydown'/'keyup')` listeners), and the course sign-up link banner, applied to every fresh HTML5Exporter output before it's considered a finished deliverable.
> - **Testing/verification discipline used throughout**: real headless-Chromium via Playwright (`sync_playwright`) for HTML5 — loading the exported file via `file://`, clicking via `canvas.click(position=...)` (element-relative, robust to per-room canvas-size changes) or `page.mouse.click()`, driving real `page.keyboard.down/up()` for movement tests, reading live JS state via `page.evaluate(...)` (e.g., `window.game.currentRoom.findFirstInstance(...)`), auto-accepting native `alert()` dialogs (HTML5's `show_message` implementation) via `page.on("dialog", lambda d: d.accept())`. For desktop: real `GameRunner`/`runtime/run_game.py` subprocess runs with `PYGM_MAX_FRAMES`/`PYGM_SCREENSHOT`/`PYGM_SEED` env vars, or direct `ProjectManager.load_project()` calls to confirm a project folder loads correctly.

Deployed. Summary of what was fixed:

**Root cause found and fixed:** `engine.js`'s collision math (`getBoundingBox`, `_movementBlocker`, and related methods) ignored sprites' `bbox_left/top/right/bottom` fields, always using the full sprite frame for collision — unlike the desktop runtime, which already used the narrower inset box. The raycast player sprite defines an 8×8 collision box centered in its 16×16 frame; on HTML5 it was using the full 16×16, giving it half the clearance near walls that desktop has. That made it much more likely to end up already overlapping a wall, which triggers the (intentional, shared) "already overlapping — let it escape" anti-freeze rule and never re-engages blocking — walking straight through the wall with no way back.

Fixed in `export/HTML5/templates/engine.js` (commit `08a3bb0`, pushed to `main`), verified via real headless Chromium showing the collision box now matches desktop exactly (`{x:44,y:428,w:8,h:8}` vs. the old `{x:40,y:424,w:16,h:16}`), with 7 new regression tests and the full suite green (3771 passed).

I also gave the promo game's own raycast player sprite (`spr_rc_person`, which had no `bbox_*` at all) that same inset box, matching the convention the official raycast samples use — cutting its collision footprint on both engines and adding real margin against wall corners. Re-exported the promo game from your live project, verified all six levels navigate cleanly with zero console errors, confirmed the raycast player's collision box is now correctly narrow, and deployed the updated build to `export_web/`.

### Claude

This is the exact bug — `refresh_object_sprites` evicts the sprite pixmap cache but not `origin_cache`. Let's check the ide_window.py call site and who calls `refresh_object_sprites`.

Now let's add the fix. I'll insert a call that refreshes open room editors whenever a sprite asset is saved, so cached origin/pixmap data doesn't go stale:

All three tests pass, confirming the fix mechanism end-to-end. Now let's run the full suite.

I'll wait for the background test notification instead. Meanwhile, this confirms the fix directly explains the user's exact scenario — the sprite's origin is already correctly saved on disk; only the already-open room editor's cache was stale.

The background test task will notify me when done — no need to poll. Let's commit the fix now since the targeted tests already passed; I'll confirm the full suite once the notification arrives.

I'll hold off committing until the full suite confirms green.

Full suite green: 3774 passed (up from 3771 — the 3 new tests), 0 failed. Let's commit and push.

**Root cause found:** the room editor caches each object's sprite pixmap *and* sprite origin (`RoomCanvas.sprite_cache`/`origin_cache`), refreshed only when a room editor tab is opened or switched. Editing a sprite's own origin from the Sprite Editor never told an already-open room editor to drop that cache — only changing *which* sprite an object uses did. So if the room editor tab (or the plateforme room) was already open while you set the flag's origin to (0, 16), it kept drawing and hit-testing with the stale (0, 0) origin.

I confirmed your `spr_pf_flag.json` already has the correct `origin_x: 0, origin_y: 16` on disk — the data was fine, only the already-open editor's cache was stale.

**Fix (pushed to `main`, commit `a2d1e5c`):** saving any sprite now refreshes every open room editor's cached geometry, matching what already happened when you reassigned an object's sprite. Full suite green (3774 passed, 0 failed). Close and reopen the room editor tab (or just re-save the sprite now, since the fix makes future saves push through automatically) and the flag should place correctly against the origin you set.

Found the actual issue — it's not something I can just patch, so here's what's going on and what I changed:

**Why Space doesn't break the gems:** the block-world's movement isn't wall-collision-based like the other levels — it's a heightmap "auto-step" system (climb up to 1 block automatically, like a stair). Since each gem sits exactly one block above the surrounding floor, the player just **auto-climbs onto it** instead of colliding with it as an obstacle. I confirmed this directly: even standing right next to a gem and facing it, `break_block` never registered a hit on it. Digging into it further would mean changing how block-breaking's aim-ray works, which is a bigger, riskier engine change than this warrants right now.

**What I changed instead:** the gems were already being collected correctly by proximity (walking onto/near them awards +10 automatically — I verified this with a real simulated walk, frame by frame, using the actual held-key movement code, and it works). The bug was just that the message told you to "press Space to break a block" in the same breath as mentioning the gems, which wrongly implied you needed to mine them. I reworded it to **"Marche sur les gemmes en diamant pour les ramasser"** (walk onto the diamond gems to collect them) — Space still works for breaking the regular terrain, just not the gems.

One real limitation that remains: the diamond block stays visually in place after being collected (there's no way to make it disappear without deeper engine work), though the player ends up standing on top of it, which mostly hides it from their own view. If that bugs you, I can look at repositioning the gems as small markers embedded in a wall face instead (genuinely breakable, Minecraft-vein style) — that's more work but would give real "mine it to collect" gameplay. Let me know if you'd like that instead.

Re-verified all 6 levels navigate cleanly with zero console errors, and deployed the updated build.

## [2026-08-21] User

> when playing block world, what is the reason for having the LEFT or RIGHT arrows move sideways instead of turning?

### Claude

The reason: `move_and_collide` — the only movement action block_world exposes — takes **literal world-space deltas** (`dx`, `dy`), not a facing-relative "move forward" amount. That's different from raycast's movement, which uses `set_direction_speed(direction: facing_angle, speed: 3)` — an action that internally does the angle math (cos/sin) for you.

Block_world has no equivalent. There's also no "turn" action at all in this extension (I just checked its full action list — `set_look_pitch` exists for looking up/down, but nothing sets horizontal `facing_angle`). So even if left/right *did* rotate the camera, `move_and_collide`'s dx/dy would still always push you in the same fixed world direction regardless of which way you're facing — you'd visually spin around but keep sliding the same way, which would feel broken, not better.

Given that, binding left/right to strafing (a fixed sideways dy) was the option that's actually consistent with how movement works here: all four directions move in fixed, predictable world directions, matching a camera that never turns.

If you want real turning (rotate + walk-forward-relative-to-facing), that needs a new capability — either a block-world equivalent of `set_direction_speed`, or computing the dx/dy via trigonometry in `execute_code`, which this level was deliberately built to avoid (no Python/Pyodide dependency). Want me to add that as a proper feature?

---

## [2026-08-21] User

> Just answer do not implement
> Could we keep the simple forward / backward / right / left mouvements but have the camera do a 90° turn?

### Claude

Yes — that's actually a clean way to sidestep the trig problem entirely.

A continuous turn (rotate a few degrees per frame, like raycast does) needs `cos`/`sin` to keep recomputing "forward" as an arbitrary angle, which is what `move_and_collide`'s plain dx/dy can't do. But a 90° *snap* turn only ever has 4 possible facing states (0°/90°/180°/270°), so "which way is forward" becomes a lookup of 4 fixed dx/dy pairs, not a continuous formula. That's just branching (`if_condition`/`test_variable` on the facing value), which the action system already supports fine — no trigonometry, no `execute_code`, no Pyodide needed.

So the design would be: up/down/left/right keep working exactly as they do now (forward/back/strafe), but which literal dx/dy each one actually applies gets chosen based on a stored "facing" state (0/90/180/270) instead of being hardcoded — and a turn action just flips that state by 90° and lets the renderer's `facing_angle` follow it.

One open question before this is buildable: what triggers the turn? Right now all four arrow keys are already spoken for (movement). You'd need either a spare key/button for "turn left 90°"/"turn right 90°", or some other trigger (e.g. corners of the map auto-turning you). Do you have something in mind for that?

---

## [2026-08-21] User

> Just answer do not implement
> I was thinking that we can have a snap 90° turn that is triggered by the right or left arrow key. Then it is just forward / backward

### Claude

Yes, that works cleanly and is simpler than what I described before, since now every key has one job:

- **Left/right**: snap the facing state 90° (0°→90°→180°→270°→0°...), and set `facing_angle` to match so the camera visually turns to face that direction.
- **Up/down**: move forward/backward *relative to the current facing* — resolved by branching on the facing state (4 cases: facing 0° → dx=+4,dy=0; facing 90° → dx=0,dy=+4; etc.) and feeding the right dx/dy into `move_and_collide`.

Both pieces are just `if_condition`/`test_variable` branching on a stored facing value — no trig, no `execute_code`. This is exactly the classic dungeon-crawler movement scheme (Wizardry/Eye of the Beholder style): turn in place, then walk in the direction you're facing.

One real tradeoff worth confirming before you commit to it: right now, movement is free — you can hold e.g. "up" and "left" together and walk diagonally-ish, since up/down and left/right drive independent world axes. With this scheme, movement becomes strictly one-axis-at-a-time in whatever direction you're currently facing — no more sideways strafing while facing forward. You'd have to turn to face a direction before you can walk that way. That's a real gameplay change, not just a control relabeling — more deliberate/puzzle-like, less free-roaming. Also the turn will be an instant snap (view pops 90°), not a smooth animated rotation, which fits the retro feel but is worth knowing going in.

If that tradeoff sounds right for this level, say so and I'll implement it.
